In both cases \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hbox {Tr}[\cdot ]$$\end{document}Tr[·] is the canonical trace on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {B}(\mathcal {H})$$\end{document}B(H) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|X|:=\sqrt{X^*X}.$$\end{document}|X|:=X∗X. Note that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {S}_\infty (\mathcal {H})$$\end{document}S∞(H) coincides with the set of all compact operators endowed with the operator norm and that in finite dimensions we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {S}_\infty (\mathcal {H})=\mathcal {B}(\mathcal {H})$$\end{document}S∞(H)=B(H).

We will be denoting the partial trace as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hbox {tr}_Q[\cdot ]:\mathcal {S}_1(\mathcal {H}_{QR})\rightarrow \mathcal {S}_1(\mathcal {H}_{R})$$\end{document}trQ[·]:S1(HQR)→S1(HR).

A trace-normalized, positive semidefinite, Schatten-1 operator is called a quantum state. We will usually denote such operator with lower case greek letters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho ,\sigma ,\omega $$\end{document}ρ,σ,ω. We denote the set of all quantum states over some Hilbert space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}$$\end{document}H as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {D}(\mathcal {H}):=\{\rho \in \mathcal {S}_1(\mathcal {H} )|\hbox {Tr}[\rho ]=1, \rho \ge 0\}$$\end{document}D(H):={ρ∈S1(H)|Tr[ρ]=1,ρ≥0}.

A linear map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi :\mathcal {B}(\mathcal {H})\rightarrow \mathcal {B}(\mathcal {K})$$\end{document}Φ:B(H)→B(K) is completely positive (CP) if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{id}\,}}_{\mathcal {B}(\mathbb {C}^n)}\otimes \Phi \in \mathcal {B}(\mathbb {C}^n\otimes \mathcal {H},\mathbb {C}^n\otimes \mathcal {K})$$\end{document}idB(Cn)⊗Φ∈B(Cn⊗H,Cn⊗K) is a positive map for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n\in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}n∈N. It is trace preserving (TP) if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hbox {Tr}[\Phi (X)]=\hbox {Tr}[X]$$\end{document}Tr[Φ(X)]=Tr[X] \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\forall X\in \mathcal {S}_1(\mathcal {H})$$\end{document}∀X∈S1(H). A quantum channel is defined as the restriction of a linear CPTP map to some state space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {D}(\mathcal {H})$$\end{document}D(H), i.e. it is an affine CPTP map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi :\mathcal {D}(\mathcal {H}_Q)\rightarrow \mathcal {D}(\mathcal {H}_R)$$\end{document}Φ:D(HQ)→D(HR). Its adjoint, denoted by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi ^*:\mathcal {B}(\mathcal {H}_R)\rightarrow \mathcal {B}(\mathcal {H}_Q)$$\end{document}Φ∗:B(HR)→B(HQ) is a linear, CP, unital (U) map, i.e. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi ^*(\mathbb {1}_R)=\mathbb {1}_Q$$\end{document}Φ∗(1R)=1Q. We denote the identity map as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{id}\,}}_S:\mathcal {B}(\mathcal {H}_S)\rightarrow \mathcal {B}(\mathcal {H}_S)$$\end{document}idS:B(HS)→B(HS). To simplify notations, we also write \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi :Q\rightarrow R$$\end{document}Φ:Q→R as a shorthand for the map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi :\mathcal {B}(\mathcal {H}_Q)\rightarrow \mathcal {B}(\mathcal {H}_R)$$\end{document}Φ:B(HQ)→B(HR).

Operator spaces

In the following, we give a concise introduction into operator space theory and operator-valued Schatten norms. These will be a central tool to derive our chain rules and additivity results in Sects. 3 and 4. For a more complete review of operator space theory or operator valued Schatten spaces, see [6] or the books [20, 21].

Operator spaces originate from the study of non-commutative geometry. They are essentially concerned with the problem of providing natural norms on spaces of vector-valued matrices and the study of the resulting structures. Since we are studying composite quantum systems, we are concerned with matrix or operator-valued matrices, which is a prime application of operator space theory.

In the following, we let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X}\subset \mathcal {B}(\mathcal {K})$$\end{document}X⊂B(K) be a linear subspace. Then we construct a “natural” family of norms on the spaces\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} M_{m,n}(\mathcal {X}):=\Big \{[X_{ij}]_{i\in [m],j\in [n]}|X_{ij}\in \mathcal {X}\Big \} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Mm,n(X):={[Xij]i∈[m],j∈[n]|Xij∈X}of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X}$$\end{document}X-valued \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m\times n$$\end{document}m×n matrices. For simplicity we write \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_{n}(\mathcal {X})\equiv M_{n,n}(\mathcal {X})$$\end{document}Mn(X)≡Mn,n(X). We construct norms on these spaces by viewing elements of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_{m,n}(\mathcal {X})$$\end{document}Mm,n(X) as linear maps in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {B}(\mathcal {K}^n,\mathcal {K}^m)$$\end{document}B(Kn,Km), where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {K}^n:=\bigoplus _{i=1}^n\mathcal {K}=\mathbb {C}^n\otimes \mathcal {K}$$\end{document}Kn:=⨁i=1nK=Cn⊗K, via the identification \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_{m,n}(\mathcal {X})\subset M_{m,n}(\mathcal {B}(\mathcal {K}))\simeq \mathcal {B}(\mathcal {K}^n,\mathcal {K}^m)$$\end{document}Mm,n(X)⊂Mm,n(B(K))≃B(Kn,Km):\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} X=[X_{ij}]\in M_{m,n}(\mathcal {X}) \leftrightarrow X:\mathcal {K}^n \rightarrow \mathcal {K}^m;\quad v^n=[v_1,\dots , v_n]^{\textrm{T}}\mapsto \Big [\sum _j X_{ij}v_j\Big ]^{\textrm{T}}\,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}X=[Xij]∈Mm,n(X)↔X:Kn→Km;vn=[v1,⋯,vn]T↦[∑jXijvj]T.Hence the space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_{m,n}(\mathcal {B}(\mathcal {K}))$$\end{document}Mm,n(B(K)) is naturally equipped with the norms induced by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {B}(\mathcal {K}^n,\mathcal {K}^m)$$\end{document}B(Kn,Km), i.e.\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert X\Vert _{m,n}\equiv \Vert X\Vert _{M_{m,n}(\mathcal {X})}&:=\sup \{\Vert Xv^n\Vert _{\mathcal {K}^m}|v^n\in \mathcal {K}^n, \Vert v^n\Vert _{\mathcal {K}^n}\le 1\} \\ &= \sup \left\{ \left( \sum _{i=1}^m\bigg \Vert \sum _{j=1}^nX_{ij}v_j\bigg \Vert _{\mathcal {K}}^2\right) ^{\frac{1}{2}}\Bigg |\sum _{j=1}^n\Vert v_j\Vert _{\mathcal {K}}^2=1\right\} , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖X‖m,n≡‖X‖Mm,n(X):=sup{‖Xvn‖Km|vn∈Kn,‖vn‖Kn≤1}=sup∑i=1m‖∑j=1nXijvj‖K212|∑j=1n‖vj‖K2=1,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert \cdot \Vert _{\mathcal {K}}$$\end{document}‖·‖K denotes the norm on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {K}$$\end{document}K. In the case \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m=n$$\end{document}m=n, we write \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert \cdot \Vert _n:=\Vert \cdot \Vert _{n,n}$$\end{document}‖·‖n:=‖·‖n,n. For simplicity we will also denote \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert X\Vert _{\mathcal {X}}\equiv \Vert X\Vert _{M_1(\mathcal {X})}$$\end{document}‖X‖X≡‖X‖M1(X).

Proposition 2.1

The family of norms defined above satisfies the following two main properties, namely for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m,n\in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}m,n∈N,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \text {(i)} \quad&\Vert FXG\Vert _m \le \Vert F\Vert _{m,n}\Vert X\Vert _n\Vert G\Vert _{n,m} \quad \forall F,G^*\in M_{m,n}(\mathbb {C}), X\in M_n(\mathcal {X}), \\ \text {(ii)} \quad&\Vert X\oplus Y\Vert _{m+n} = \max \{\Vert X\Vert _n,\Vert Y\Vert _m\} \quad \forall X\in M_n(\mathcal {X}), Y\in M_m(\mathcal {X}), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(i)‖FXG‖m≤‖F‖m,n‖X‖n‖G‖n,m∀F,G∗∈Mm,n(C),X∈Mn(X),(ii)‖X⊕Y‖m+n=max{‖X‖n,‖Y‖m}∀X∈Mn(X),Y∈Mm(X),where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X\oplus Y= \begin{pmatrix} X & 0\\ 0 & Y \end{pmatrix}$$\end{document}X⊕Y=X00Y, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$FXG\equiv (F\otimes \mathbb {1}_\mathcal {K})X(G\otimes \mathbb {1}_\mathcal {K})$$\end{document}FXG≡(F⊗1K)X(G⊗1K).

More generally,

Definition 2.2

A linear space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X}$$\end{document}X with a family of norms \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert \cdot \Vert _{m,n}$$\end{document}‖·‖m,n on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_{m,n}(\mathcal {X})$$\end{document}Mm,n(X) that satisfy the above properties (i) and (ii) is called an (abstract) operator space.

It turns out that the only linear spaces \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X}$$\end{document}X with endowed norms \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert \cdot \Vert _{m,n}$$\end{document}‖·‖m,n on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_{m,n}(\mathcal {X})$$\end{document}Mm,n(X) satisfying properties (i) and (ii) are closed linear subspaces of some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {B}(\mathcal {K})$$\end{document}B(K), where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {K}$$\end{document}K is a (possibly infinite dimensional) Hilbert space. Hence closed linear subspaces \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X}\subset \mathcal {B}(\mathcal {K})$$\end{document}X⊂B(K) are called (concrete) operator spaces.

Norms on operator-valued Schatten spaces

Next we describe the operator space structure of operator-valued Schatten norms, sometimes also referred to as amalgamated \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_p$$\end{document}Lp norms or Pisier norms. For simplicity of introduction we let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}$$\end{document}H be a Hilbert space of dimension \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d<\infty $$\end{document}d<∞ here. Given some operator space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X}$$\end{document}X, we set\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {S}_\infty [\mathcal {H},\mathcal {X}]:=M_d(\mathcal {X}), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}S∞[H,X]:=Md(X),i.e. the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d\times d$$\end{document}d×d matrices taking values in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X}$$\end{document}X, with the norm \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert \cdot \Vert _d\equiv \Vert \cdot \Vert _{M_{d}(\mathcal {X})}$$\end{document}‖·‖d≡‖·‖Md(X). It is an operator space since we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} M_{m,n}(\mathcal {S}_\infty [\mathcal {H},\mathcal {X}]) \simeq M_{md,nd}(\mathcal {X}), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Mm,n(S∞[H,X])≃Mmd,nd(X),which clearly satisfy Proposition 2.1 (i), (ii). We remark here that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {S}_\infty [\mathcal {H},\mathbb {C}]=\mathcal {S}_\infty (\mathcal {H})$$\end{document}S∞[H,C]=S∞(H) follows directly from the definition above.

The main technical tool in this work are the operator space norms of Schatten-q-spaces of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X}$$\end{document}X-valued operators \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {S}_p[\mathcal {H},\mathcal {X}]$$\end{document}Sp[H,X]. Since a complete description of the operator space structure of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {S}_p[\mathcal {H},\mathcal {X}]$$\end{document}Sp[H,X] is beyond the scope of the present paper, we refer to [6, 10] for more details. For their original construction via interpolation between certain Haagerup-tensor products of row- and column-operator spaces, see [20]. Remarkably, we can omit this because we are able to define and work with these norms by only understanding the norm on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {S}_\infty [\mathcal {H},\mathcal {X}]$$\end{document}S∞[H,X] discussed above. Next, we enumerate some of their core properties.

In the case where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}$$\end{document}H is finite dimensional, these operator-valued Schatten spaces should be thought of as linear spaces of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d\times d$$\end{document}d×d matrices valued in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X}$$\end{document}X, with special norms \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert \cdot \Vert _{\mathcal {S}_q[\mathcal {H},\mathcal {X}]}$$\end{document}‖·‖Sq[H,X]. These extend naturally to infinite dimensional settings. Importantly, in the case where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X}=\mathbb {C}$$\end{document}X=C they all reduce to the well-known Banach space of Schatten class operators [6], i.e.\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {S}_q[\mathcal {H},\mathbb {C}]= \mathcal {S}_q(\mathcal {H}). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Sq[H,C]=Sq(H).They also satisfy the following duality relation, namely5\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left( \mathcal {S}_p[\mathcal {H},\mathcal {X}]\right) ^* = \mathcal {S}_{p^\prime }[\mathcal {H},\mathcal {X}^*], \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Sp[H,X]∗=Sp′[H,X∗],where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{p^\prime }=1$$\end{document}1p+1p′=1 are dual indices and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X}^*$$\end{document}X∗ is the operator space dual of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X}$$\end{document}X [21]. Since the norms for other values of q are defined via interpolation between \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {S}_\infty [\mathcal {H},\mathcal {X}]$$\end{document}S∞[H,X] and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {S}_1[\mathcal {H},\mathcal {X}]$$\end{document}S1[H,X] (which can be defined via (5)) [20], they satisfy many desirable properties, including Pisier’s formula, which we take as their definition.

Theorem 2.3

(Pisier’s formula [20]). Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}$$\end{document}H be a separable Hilbert space and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X}$$\end{document}X an operator space. Then for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1\le p\le \infty $$\end{document}1≤p≤∞ the following variational formulas hold for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X\in \mathcal {S}_p[\mathcal {H},\mathcal {X}]$$\end{document}X∈Sp[H,X].\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert X\Vert _{\mathcal {S}_p[\mathcal {H},\mathcal {X}]}= \inf _{\underset{X=FYG}{F,G\in \mathcal {S}_{2p}(\mathcal {H}), Y\in \mathcal {S}_\infty [\mathcal {H},\mathcal {X}]}}\Vert F\Vert _{2p}\Vert Y\Vert _{\mathcal {S}_\infty [\mathcal {H},\mathcal {X}]}\Vert G\Vert _{2p}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖X‖Sp[H,X]=infF,G∈S2p(H),Y∈S∞[H,X]X=FYG‖F‖2p‖Y‖S∞[H,X]‖G‖2p.

Firstly we note that it is not mandatory that F, G are square, i.e. the statement still holds when taking the infimum over F, G, Y s.t. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F,G^*\in \mathcal {S}_{2p}(\mathcal {K},\mathcal {H})$$\end{document}F,G∗∈S2p(K,H) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Y\in \mathcal {S}_\infty [\mathcal {K},\mathcal {X}]$$\end{document}Y∈S∞[K,X] as long as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\dim \mathcal {H}\le \dim \mathcal {K}$$\end{document}dimH≤dimK. The statement in the case when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}=\mathcal {K}$$\end{document}H=K is however sufficient in practice. Next, we prove two important direct consequences of Pisier’s formula.

Corollary 2.4

1) Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X}$$\end{document}X be an operator space. Then, the norm on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_p[\mathcal {H},\mathcal {X}]$$\end{document}Sp[H,X] is invariant under local isometries \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V^*,U\in \mathcal {B}(\mathcal {H},\mathcal {K})$$\end{document}V∗,U∈B(H,K) satisfying \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U^*U=VV^*=\mathbb {1}_\mathcal {H}$$\end{document}U∗U=VV∗=1H. That is for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X\in \mathcal {S}_{p}[\mathcal {H},\mathcal {X}]$$\end{document}X∈Sp[H,X] and any such \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V^*,U\in \mathcal {B}(\mathcal {H},\mathcal {K})$$\end{document}V∗,U∈B(H,K) it holds that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert UXV\Vert _{S_p[\mathcal {K},\mathcal {X}]} = \Vert X\Vert _{S_p[\mathcal {H},\mathcal {X}]}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖UXV‖Sp[K,X]=‖X‖Sp[H,X].In particular, when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {K}=\mathcal {H}$$\end{document}K=H, this means invariance under unitaries on the first system.

2) When \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\dim \mathcal {H}<\infty $$\end{document}dimH<∞ one can restrict the infimum effectively over positive semidefinite operators, and hence\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\Vert X\Vert _{\mathcal {S}_p[\mathcal {H},\mathcal {X}]}=\inf _{F,G\in \mathcal {S}_{2p}(\mathcal {H}), F,G\ge 0}\Vert F\Vert _{2p}\Vert G\Vert _{2p}\Vert F^{-1}XG^{-1}\Vert _{\mathcal {S}_\infty [\mathcal {H},\mathcal {X}]}\\&\quad = \inf _{\underset{\Vert F\Vert _1=\Vert G\Vert _1=1}{F,G\ge 0}}\Vert F^{-\frac{1}{2p}}XG^{-\frac{1}{2p}}\Vert _{\mathcal {S}_\infty [\mathcal {H},\mathcal {X}]}\,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖X‖Sp[H,X]=infF,G∈S2p(H),F,G≥0‖F‖2p‖G‖2p‖F-1XG-1‖S∞[H,X]=infF,G≥0‖F‖1=‖G‖1=1‖F-12pXG-12p‖S∞[H,X],where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F^{-1},G^{-1}$$\end{document}F-1,G-1 denote the generalized (Moore-Penrose) inverses of F, G.

Proof

The first claim follows via isometric invariance of the Schatten norms,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert UXV\Vert _{\mathcal {S}_p[\mathcal {K},\mathcal {X}]}&= \inf _{\underset{UXV=FYG}{F,G\in \mathcal {S}_{2p}(\mathcal {K}), Y\in \mathcal {S}_\infty [\mathcal {K},\mathcal {X}]}}\Vert F\Vert _{2p}\Vert Y\Vert _{\mathcal {S}_\infty [\mathcal {K},\mathcal {X}]}\Vert G\Vert _{2p} \\ &= \inf _{\underset{X=(U^*F)Y(GV^*)}{F,G\in \mathcal {S}_{2p}(\mathcal {K}), Y\in \mathcal {S}_\infty [\mathcal {K},\mathcal {X}]}}\Vert F\Vert _{2p}\Vert Y\Vert _{\mathcal {S}_\infty [\mathcal {K},\mathcal {X}]}\Vert G\Vert _{2p} \\ &= \inf _{\underset{X=F^\prime YG^\prime }{F^\prime ,G^{\prime *}\in \mathcal {S}_{2p}(\mathcal {K},\mathcal {H}), Y\in \mathcal {S}_\infty [\mathcal {K},\mathcal {X}]}}\Vert F^\prime \Vert _{2p}\Vert Y\Vert _{\mathcal {S}_\infty [\mathcal {K},\mathcal {X}]}\Vert G^\prime \Vert _{2p} \\&= \Vert X\Vert _{\mathcal {S}_p[\mathcal {H},\mathcal {X}]}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖UXV‖Sp[K,X]=infF,G∈S2p(K),Y∈S∞[K,X]UXV=FYG‖F‖2p‖Y‖S∞[K,X]‖G‖2p=infF,G∈S2p(K),Y∈S∞[K,X]X=(U∗F)Y(GV∗)‖F‖2p‖Y‖S∞[K,X]‖G‖2p=infF′,G′∗∈S2p(K,H),Y∈S∞[K,X]X=F′YG′‖F′‖2p‖Y‖S∞[K,X]‖G′‖2p=‖X‖Sp[H,X],where in the third line we redefined \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F^\prime =U^*F\in \mathcal {S}_{2p}(\mathcal {K},\mathcal {H})$$\end{document}F′=U∗F∈S2p(K,H) and used that they have identical Schatten-2p-norms. The fourth line follows from the comment above on non-square F, G.

To prove the second statement, we let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X\in \mathcal {S}_{p}[\mathcal {H},\mathcal {X}]$$\end{document}X∈Sp[H,X], assuming \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\dim \mathcal {H}<\infty $$\end{document}dimH<∞. Then, similarly to above, observe that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert X\Vert _{\mathcal {S}_p[\mathcal {H},\mathcal {X}]}&= \inf _{\underset{F,G,Y \text { s.t. } X=FYG}{F,G\in \mathcal {S}_{2p}(\mathcal {H}), Y\in \mathcal {S}_\infty [\mathcal {H},\mathcal {X}]}}\Vert F\Vert _{2p}\Vert Y\Vert _{\mathcal {S}_\infty [\mathcal {H},\mathcal {X}]}\Vert G\Vert _{2p} \\&= \inf _{\underset{F,G,Y \text { s.t. }X=P_F(UYV)P_G}{F,G\in \mathcal {S}_{2p}(\mathcal {H}), Y\in \mathcal {S}_\infty [\mathcal {H},\mathcal {X}]}}\Vert P_F\Vert _{2p}\Vert Y\Vert _{\mathcal {S}_\infty [\mathcal {H},\mathcal {X}]}\Vert P_G\Vert _{2p} \\&= \inf _{\underset{F,G,Y \text { s.t. }X=P_FYP_G}{F,G\in \mathcal {S}_{2p}(\mathcal {H}), Y\in \mathcal {S}_\infty [\mathcal {H},\mathcal {X}]}}\Vert P_F\Vert _{2p}\Vert Y\Vert _{\mathcal {S}_\infty [\mathcal {H},\mathcal {X}]}\Vert P_G\Vert _{2p}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖X‖Sp[H,X]=infF,G∈S2p(H),Y∈S∞[H,X]F,G,Ys.t.X=FYG‖F‖2p‖Y‖S∞[H,X]‖G‖2p=infF,G∈S2p(H),Y∈S∞[H,X]F,G,Ys.t.X=PF(UYV)PG‖PF‖2p‖Y‖S∞[H,X]‖PG‖2p=infF,G∈S2p(H),Y∈S∞[H,X]F,G,Ys.t.X=PFYPG‖PF‖2p‖Y‖S∞[H,X]‖PG‖2p,where in the second line we set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F=P_FU$$\end{document}F=PFU and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G=VP_G$$\end{document}G=VPG to be the right-, respectively, left-polar decompositions of F, G, such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P_F,P_G\ge 0$$\end{document}PF,PG≥0. For the third equality we used the above and renamed UYV to Y. Denote with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P_F^{-1},P_G^{-1}$$\end{document}PF-1,PG-1 their Moore-Penrose inverses and with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Pi _F:=P_FP_F^{-1}=P_F^{-1}P_F$$\end{document}ΠF:=PFPF-1=PF-1PF the projection onto the support of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P_F$$\end{document}PF, and analogously for G. Now for a triple (F, G, Y) that occurs in the infimum we define \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{Y}:=\Pi _FY\Pi _G$$\end{document}Y~:=ΠFYΠG. Then by Proposition 2.1i), see also [20, Lemma 1.6], it follows that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert \Pi _FY\Pi _G\Vert _{\mathcal {S}_\infty [\mathcal {H},\mathcal {X}]} \le \Vert Y\Vert _{\mathcal {S}_\infty [\mathcal {H},\mathcal {X}]}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖ΠFYΠG‖S∞[H,X]≤‖Y‖S∞[H,X],since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert \Pi _F\Vert ,\Vert \Pi _G\Vert \le 1$$\end{document}‖ΠF‖,‖ΠG‖≤1. Hence it holds that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert X\Vert _{\mathcal {S}_p[\mathcal {H},\mathcal {X}]} \ge \inf _{\underset{F,G,Y \text { s.t. }X=P_FYP_G}{F,G\in \mathcal {S}_{2p}(\mathcal {H}), Y\in \mathcal {S}_\infty [\mathcal {H},\mathcal {X}]}}\Vert P_F\Vert _{2p}\Vert \Pi _FY\Pi _G\Vert _{\mathcal {S}_\infty [\mathcal {H},\mathcal {X}]}\Vert P_G\Vert _{2p}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖X‖Sp[H,X]≥infF,G∈S2p(H),Y∈S∞[H,X]F,G,Ys.t.X=PFYPG‖PF‖2p‖ΠFYΠG‖S∞[H,X]‖PG‖2p.On the other hand for any suitable triple (F, G, Y) it follows that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} X=P_FYP_G=P_F\Pi _FY\Pi _GP_G=P_F\tilde{Y}P_G, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}X=PFYPG=PFΠFYΠGPG=PFY~PG,hence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(F,G,\Pi _FY\Pi _G)$$\end{document}(F,G,ΠFYΠG) is also a compatible triple. Since we have by definition an injection from suitable triples (F, G, Y) to ones \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(F,G,\Pi _FY\Pi _G)$$\end{document}(F,G,ΠFYΠG) it follows that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert X\Vert _{\mathcal {S}_p[\mathcal {H},\mathcal {X}]} \le \inf _{\underset{ X=P_FYP_G}{F,G\in \mathcal {S}_{2p}(\mathcal {H}), Y\in \mathcal {S}_\infty [\mathcal {H},\mathcal {X}]}}\Vert P_F\Vert _{2p}\Vert \Pi _FY\Pi _G\Vert _{\mathcal {S}_\infty [\mathcal {H},\mathcal {X}]}\Vert P_G\Vert _{2p}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖X‖Sp[H,X]≤infF,G∈S2p(H),Y∈S∞[H,X]X=PFYPG‖PF‖2p‖ΠFYΠG‖S∞[H,X]‖PG‖2p.So overall we have shown equality. Now by construction we further have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} P_F^{-1}XP_G^{-1} = \Pi _FY\Pi _G=\tilde{Y} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}PF-1XPG-1=ΠFYΠG=Y~and in total we get\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert X\Vert _{\mathcal {S}_p[\mathcal {H},\mathcal {X}]}&= \inf _{P_F,P_G\in \mathcal {S}_{2p}(\mathcal {H}), P_F,P_G\ge 0}\Vert P_F\Vert _{2p}\Vert P_F^{-1}XP_G^{-1}\Vert _{\mathcal {S}_\infty [\mathcal {H},\mathcal {X}]}\Vert P_G\Vert _{2p} \\&=\inf _{\underset{\Vert F\Vert _1=\Vert G\Vert _1=1}{F,G\ge 0}}\Vert F^{-\frac{1}{2p}}XG^{-\frac{1}{2p}}\Vert _{\mathcal {S}_\infty [\mathcal {H},\mathcal {X}]}\,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖X‖Sp[H,X]=infPF,PG∈S2p(H),PF,PG≥0‖PF‖2p‖PF-1XPG-1‖S∞[H,X]‖PG‖2p=infF,G≥0‖F‖1=‖G‖1=1‖F-12pXG-12p‖S∞[H,X],which is what we wanted to show. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Although it is not obvious from the above, the expression in Theorem 2.3 does define a norm. In particular, it satisfies the triangle inequality, Hölder’s duality, and the property that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert X\Vert _{\mathcal {S}_q[\mathcal {H},\mathcal {S}_q(\mathcal {K})]}=\Vert X\Vert _{\mathcal {S}_q(\mathcal {H}\otimes \mathcal {K})}$$\end{document}‖X‖Sq[H,Sq(K)]=‖X‖Sq(H⊗K). This last property follows from the more general fact that for two Hilbert spaces \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}, \mathcal {K}$$\end{document}H,K6\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {S}_q[\mathcal {H},\mathcal {S}_q[\mathcal {K},\mathcal {X}]] \simeq \mathcal {S}_q[\mathcal {H}\otimes \mathcal {K},\mathcal {X}]\simeq \mathcal {S}_q[\mathcal {K},\mathcal {S}_q[\mathcal {H},\mathcal {X}]], \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Sq[H,Sq[K,X]]≃Sq[H⊗K,X]≃Sq[K,Sq[H,X]],where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\simeq $$\end{document}≃ means they are completely isomorphic, see Sect. 2.4, i.e. equal as operator spaces [20, Theorem 1.9].

Pisier’s Theorem 2.3 was also used to give tractable variational expressions for the norms on the spaces \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {S}_q[\mathcal {H},\mathcal {S}_p(\mathcal {K})]$$\end{document}Sq[H,Sp(K)], see e.g. the case \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X}=\mathcal {S}_p[\mathcal {K},\mathbb {C}]=\mathcal {S}_p(\mathcal {K})$$\end{document}X=Sp[K,C]=Sp(K) [10, Section 3.5]. These make the operator-valued Schatten norms with two indices very tractable for applications in quantum information theory, see e.g. [5–8, 10, 12, 13, 27].

Theorem 2.5

([20]). Given an element \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X\in \mathcal {S}_q[\mathcal {H}_1,\mathcal {S}_p(\mathcal {H}_2)]$$\end{document}X∈Sq[H1,Sp(H2)] acting on the Hilbert space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}_1\otimes \mathcal {H}_2$$\end{document}H1⊗H2 it holds that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert X_{12}\Vert _{\mathcal {S}_q[\mathcal {H}_1,\mathcal {S}_p(\mathcal {H}_2)]} = {\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} \inf _{\underset{X_{12}=F_1Y_{12}G_1}{F,G\in \mathcal {S}_{2r}(\mathcal {H}_1),Y\in \mathcal {S}_{p}(\mathcal {H}_{12})}} \Vert F\Vert _{2r}\Vert G\Vert _{2r}\Vert Y\Vert _{p}\,, & \quad for q\le p \\ \Vert X_{12}\Vert _p\,, & \quad for q=p \\ \sup _{F,G\in \mathcal {S}_{2r}(\mathcal {H}_1)} \Vert F\Vert ^{-1}_{2r}\Vert G\Vert ^{-1}_{2r}\Vert F_1X_{12}G_1\Vert _p, & \quad for q\ge p \end{array}\right. } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖X12‖Sq[H1,Sp(H2)]=infF,G∈S2r(H1),Y∈Sp(H12)X12=F1Y12G1‖F‖2r‖G‖2r‖Y‖p,forq≤p‖X12‖p,forq=psupF,G∈S2r(H1)‖F‖2r-1‖G‖2r-1‖F1X12G1‖p,forq≥pwhere the infimum and supremum are over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F,G\in \mathcal {S}_{2r}(\mathcal {H}_1)$$\end{document}F,G∈S2r(H1) acting only on the first Hilbert space and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Y\in \mathcal {S}_q(\mathcal {H}_{12})$$\end{document}Y∈Sq(H12) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{1}{r}:=\left| \frac{1}{q}-\frac{1}{p}\right| $$\end{document}1r:=1q-1p. Further if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X_{12}\ge 0$$\end{document}X12≥0, then one can choose \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F=G$$\end{document}F=G, see [10] or [5, Proposition 3.1 (v,vi)] and [4, Proposition 5.2 iii)].

Some of their central properties are summarized in [10]. In Lemma 3.1 below, we show that one can also obtain a version of these for the more general case where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {S}_q(\mathcal {H})$$\end{document}Sq(H) is replaced by any other operator space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X}$$\end{document}X.

Notation 1

(multi-index Schatten norms) We introduce the following notation to keep track of the Schatten indices occurring in the norms, their order, value, and associated quantum systems. Given an operator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X\in \mathcal {S}_q[\mathcal {H}_A,\mathcal {S}_p[\mathcal {H}_B,\mathcal {S}_r(\mathcal {H}_C)]]$$\end{document}X∈Sq[HA,Sp[HB,Sr(HC)]] acting on the tripartite quantum system \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}_A\otimes \mathcal {H}_B\otimes \mathcal {H}_C$$\end{document}HA⊗HB⊗HC we write\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert X\Vert _{(A:q,B:p,C:r)} \equiv \Vert X\Vert _{\mathcal {S}_q[\mathcal {H}_A,\mathcal {S}_p[\mathcal {H}_B,\mathcal {S}_r(\mathcal {H}_C)]]} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖X‖(A:q,B:p,C:r)≡‖X‖Sq[HA,Sp[HB,Sr(HC)]]and more generally for a suitable \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X\in \mathcal {B}(\otimes _{i=1}^k\mathcal {H}_{A_i})$$\end{document}X∈B(⊗i=1kHAi)\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert X\Vert _{(A_1:q_1...\,A_k:q_k)} \equiv \Vert X\Vert _{\mathcal {S}_{q_1}[\mathcal {H}_{A_1}...\,\mathcal {S}_{q_k}(\mathcal {H}_{A_k})...]}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖X‖(A1:q1...Ak:qk)≡‖X‖Sq1[HA1...Sqk(HAk)...].Likewise, for an operator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X\in \mathcal {S}_q[\mathcal {H}_A,\mathcal {S}_p[\mathcal {H}_B,\mathcal {X}]]$$\end{document}X∈Sq[HA,Sp[HB,X]] we will write\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert X\Vert _{(A:q,B:p;\mathcal {X})} \equiv \Vert X\Vert _{\mathcal {S}_q[\mathcal {H}_A,\,\mathcal {S}_p[\mathcal {H}_B,\mathcal {X}]]} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖X‖(A:q,B:p;X)≡‖X‖Sq[HA,Sp[HB,X]]and analogously for a different number of indices. Importantly, note also that the order in which the systems appear is determined by the order of the indices in the norm, and whenever possible we will try to make the order of systems in the operator match.

That is, formally we have in this notation for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X\in \mathcal {B}(\mathcal {H}_A)$$\end{document}X∈B(HA) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Y\in \mathcal {B}(\mathcal {H}_B)$$\end{document}Y∈B(HB),\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert X\otimes Y\Vert _{(A:q,B:p)} = \Vert Y\otimes X\Vert _{(A:q,B:p)} = \Vert X\Vert _q\Vert Y\Vert _p\,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖X⊗Y‖(A:q,B:p)=‖Y⊗X‖(A:q,B:p)=‖X‖q‖Y‖p,but we try to avoid the notation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert Y\otimes X\Vert _{(A:q,B:p)}$$\end{document}‖Y⊗X‖(A:q,B:p) as much as possible to avoid confusions.

The following is a direct consequence of (6):

Proposition 2.6

Consecutive Schatten indices of the same value can be combined:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert X\Vert _{(A_1:q_1,...,A_{m-1}:q_{m-1},A_{m}:p,...,A_{m+n}:p;\mathcal {X})} = \Vert X\Vert _{(A_1:q_1...,A_{m-1}:q_{m-1},A_{m}\cdots A_{m+n}:p;\mathcal {X})}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖X‖(A1:q1,...,Am-1:qm-1,Am:p,...,Am+n:p;X)=‖X‖(A1:q1...,Am-1:qm-1,Am⋯Am+n:p;X),This in particular implies that if all Schatten indices are equal, then the norm\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert X\Vert _{(A_1:p...\,A_k:p)}=\Vert X\Vert _p \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖X‖(A1:p...Ak:p)=‖X‖preduces to the Schatten-p-norm of X.

In the next proposition, we show that operator-valued Schatten norms enjoy a natural multiplicativity property.

Proposition 2.7

For any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A\otimes X\in S_q[\mathcal {H},\mathcal {X}]$$\end{document}A⊗X∈Sq[H,X],\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert A\otimes X\Vert _{S_q[\mathcal {H},\mathcal {X}]}=\Vert A\Vert _q\Vert X\Vert _{\mathcal {X}}\,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖A⊗X‖Sq[H,X]=‖A‖q‖X‖X.More generally for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bigotimes _{i=1}^k X_i\in \mathcal {S}_{q_1}[\mathcal {H}_{A_1}[\mathcal {S}_{q_2}[...\,\mathcal {S}_{q_k}(\mathcal {H}_{A_k})]...]]$$\end{document}⨂i=1kXi∈Sq1[HA1[Sq2[...Sqk(HAk)]...]],\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \bigg \Vert \bigotimes _{i=1}^k X_i\bigg \Vert _{(A_1:q_1,...,A_k:q_k)}= \prod _{i=1}^k \Vert X_i\Vert _{(A_i:q_i)}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖⨂i=1kXi‖(A1:q1,...,Ak:qk)=∏i=1k‖Xi‖(Ai:qi).

Proof

In case that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d:=\dim \mathcal {H}<\infty $$\end{document}d:=dimH<∞ the first statement follows from Corollary 2.4:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert A\otimes X\Vert _{S_q[\mathcal {H},\mathcal {X}]}&= \inf _{F,G\in \mathcal {S}_{2q}(\mathcal {H}), F,G\ge 0}\Vert F\Vert _{2q}\Vert G\Vert _{2q}\Vert F^{-1}AG^{-1}\otimes X\Vert _{\mathcal {S}_\infty [\mathcal {H},\mathcal {X}]} \\&=\inf _{F,G\in \mathcal {S}_{2q}(\mathcal {H}), F,G\ge 0}\Vert F\Vert _{2q}\Vert G\Vert _{2q}\Vert F^{-1}AG^{-1}\Vert _\infty \Vert X\Vert _{\mathcal {X}} \\&= \Vert A\Vert _q\Vert X\Vert _\mathcal {X}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖A⊗X‖Sq[H,X]=infF,G∈S2q(H),F,G≥0‖F‖2q‖G‖2q‖F-1AG-1⊗X‖S∞[H,X]=infF,G∈S2q(H),F,G≥0‖F‖2q‖G‖2q‖F-1AG-1‖∞‖X‖X=‖A‖q‖X‖X.Here in the second line we used that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert B\otimes X\Vert _{S_\infty [\mathcal {H},\mathcal {X}]}\!=\!\Vert B\otimes X\Vert _{M_d(\mathcal {X})}\!=\!\Vert B\Vert _{M_d(\mathbb {C})}\Vert X\Vert _\mathcal {X}= \Vert B\Vert _\infty \Vert X\Vert _\mathcal {X}$$\end{document}‖B⊗X‖S∞[H,X]=‖B⊗X‖Md(X)=‖B‖Md(C)‖X‖X=‖B‖∞‖X‖X, which follows from Proposition 2.1 and the definition of the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_d(\mathcal {X})$$\end{document}Md(X) norm. Alternatively this also follows by assuming without loss of generality that A is block-diagonal, since else we may absorb SVD-unitaries into the norm by Corollary 2.4, and applying [20, Corollary 1.3]. The second statement now follows directly by induction:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \bigg \Vert \bigotimes _{i=1}^k X_i\bigg \Vert _{(A_1:q_1,...,A_k:q_k)}&= \bigg \Vert X_1\otimes \bigotimes _{i=2}^{k} X_i\bigg \Vert _{S_{q_1}[\mathcal {H}_{A_1},\,\mathcal {X}]} = \Vert X_1\Vert _{q_1} \bigg \Vert \bigotimes _{i=2}^k X_i\bigg \Vert _{(A_2:q_2,...,A_k:q_k)}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖⨂i=1kXi‖(A1:q1,...,Ak:qk)=‖X1⊗⨂i=2kXi‖Sq1[HA1,X]=‖X1‖q1‖⨂i=2kXi‖(A2:q2,...,Ak:qk),where we used \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X}=S_{q_2}[\mathcal {H}_{A_2}...\,\mathcal {S}_{q_k}(\mathcal {H}_{A_k})]...]$$\end{document}X=Sq2[HA2...Sqk(HAk)]...]. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Norms on linear maps

For a linear map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi : \mathcal {S}_{q_1}[\mathcal {H}_{A_1}...\,\mathcal {S}_{q_k}(\mathcal {H}_{A_k})...]\rightarrow \mathcal {S}_{p_1}[\mathcal {H}_{B_1}...\,$$\end{document}Φ:Sq1[HA1...Sqk(HAk)...]→Sp1[HB1... \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {S}_{p_l}(\mathcal {H}_{B_l})...]$$\end{document}Spl(HBl)...] we set\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert \Phi \Vert _{(A_1:q_1...\,A_k:q_k)\rightarrow (B_1:p_1...\,B_l:p_l)}:=\sup _{X\ne 0} \frac{\Vert \Phi (X)\Vert _{(B_1:p_1...\,B_l:p_l)}}{\Vert X\Vert _{(A_1:q_1...\,A_k:q_k)}}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖Φ‖(A1:q1...Ak:qk)→(B1:p1...Bl:pl):=supX≠0‖Φ(X)‖(B1:p1...Bl:pl)‖X‖(A1:q1...Ak:qk),where the supremum is over all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X\in \mathcal {S}_{q_1}[\mathcal {H}_{A_1}...\,\mathcal {S}_{q_k}(\mathcal {H}_{A_k})...]$$\end{document}X∈Sq1[HA1...Sqk(HAk)...]. We write a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$^+$$\end{document}+ superscript next to its channel norm when we restrict the optimization over positive semidefinite operators, i.e.\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert \Phi \Vert ^+_{(A_1:q_1...\,A_k:q_k)\rightarrow (B_1:p_1...\,B_l:p_l)}:=\sup _{X\ge 0, X\ne 0} \frac{\Vert \Phi (X)\Vert _{(B_1:p_1...\,B_l:p_l)}}{\Vert X\Vert _{(A_1:q_1...\,A_k:q_k)}}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖Φ‖(A1:q1...Ak:qk)→(B1:p1...Bl:pl)+:=supX≥0,X≠0‖Φ(X)‖(B1:p1...Bl:pl)‖X‖(A1:q1...Ak:qk).This is useful when taking these maps as quantum channels acting on (positive) quantum states. For most channel norms of interest in our applications it is known that for a CP map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi $$\end{document}Φ, one can restrict the optimization over positive elements only without changing the norm, i.e. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert \Phi \Vert =\Vert \Phi \Vert ^+$$\end{document}‖Φ‖=‖Φ‖+, see Lemma A.2.

The completely bounded (cb) norm of a linear map is defined as\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\Vert \Phi \Vert _{cb,(A_1:q_1...\,A_k:q_k)\rightarrow (B_1:p_1...\,B_l:p_l)}\\&\quad := \sup _E\Vert {{\,\textrm{id}\,}}_E\otimes \Phi \Vert _{(E:\infty ,A_1:q_1...\,A_k:q_k)\rightarrow (E:\infty ,B_1:p_1...\,B_l:p_l)}\,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖Φ‖cb,(A1:q1...Ak:qk)→(B1:p1...Bl:pl):=supE‖idE⊗Φ‖(E:∞,A1:q1...Ak:qk)→(E:∞,B1:p1...Bl:pl),where the supremum is over environment systems E of arbitrary size. It was shown in [20, Lemma 1.7], however, that the cb-norm is independent of the index of the environment E,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert \Phi \Vert _{cb,(A_1:q_1...\,A_k:q_k)\rightarrow (B_1:p_1...\,B_l:p_l)} = \sup _E\Vert {{\,\textrm{id}\,}}_E\otimes \Phi \Vert _{(E:t,A_1:q_1...\,A_k:q_k)\rightarrow (E:t,B_1:p_1...\,B_l:p_l)}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖Φ‖cb,(A1:q1...Ak:qk)→(B1:p1...Bl:pl)=supE‖idE⊗Φ‖(E:t,A1:q1...Ak:qk)→(E:t,B1:p1...Bl:pl),for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1\le t\le \infty $$\end{document}1≤t≤∞. We will be choosing this index at our convenience. One immediate consequence of this is that for any linear map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi :\mathcal {X}\rightarrow \mathcal {Y}$$\end{document}Φ:X→Y between operator spaces it holds that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert {{\,\textrm{id}\,}}_A\otimes \Phi \Vert _{cb,\mathcal {S}_q[\mathcal {H}_A,\mathcal {X}]\rightarrow \mathcal {S}_q[\mathcal {H}_A,\mathcal {Y}]} = \Vert \Phi \Vert _{cb,\mathcal {X}\rightarrow \mathcal {Y}} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖idA⊗Φ‖cb,Sq[HA,X]→Sq[HA,Y]=‖Φ‖cb,X→Yfor any quantum system A.

A linear map between operator spaces is called a complete isometry if it is invertible and the map and its inverse both have a CB norm equal to 1. A well-known occurrence of the CB norm in quantum information theory is the diamond norm: for a difference of channels \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi -\Psi :A\rightarrow B$$\end{document}Φ-Ψ:A→B\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert \Phi -\Psi \Vert _\diamond = \sup _E\,\Vert {{\,\textrm{id}\,}}_E\otimes (\Phi -\Psi )\Vert _{(EA:1)\rightarrow (EB:1)} = \Vert \Phi -\Psi \Vert _{cb,(A:1)\rightarrow (B:1)}\,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖Φ-Ψ‖⋄=supE‖idE⊗(Φ-Ψ)‖(EA:1)→(EB:1)=‖Φ-Ψ‖cb,(A:1)→(B:1).Of important interest to the present discussion is the SWAP map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F_{A\leftrightarrow B}:\mathcal {B}(\mathcal {H}_A\otimes \mathcal {H}_B)\rightarrow \mathcal {B}(\mathcal {H}_B\otimes \mathcal {H}_A): X_A\otimes X_B\mapsto X_B\otimes X_A$$\end{document}FA↔B:B(HA⊗HB)→B(HB⊗HA):XA⊗XB↦XB⊗XA. It is is a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textit{complete contraction}$$\end{document}complete contraction when acting on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {S}_p[\mathcal {H}_A,\mathcal {S}_q(\mathcal {H}_B)]$$\end{document}Sp[HA,Sq(HB)] for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q\ge p$$\end{document}q≥p [10, Theorem 8]. This means that for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q\ge p$$\end{document}q≥p, any t and system E:7\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert {{\,\textrm{id}\,}}_E\otimes F_{A \leftrightarrow B}\Vert _{(E:t,A:p,B:q)\rightarrow (E:t,B:q,A:p)} \le \Vert F_{A \leftrightarrow B}\Vert _{cb,(A:p,B:q)\rightarrow (B:q,A:p)} \le 1\,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖idE⊗FA↔B‖(E:t,A:p,B:q)→(E:t,B:q,A:p)≤‖FA↔B‖cb,(A:p,B:q)→(B:q,A:p)≤1.This implies that, in our notations \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert X\Vert _{(B:q,A:p)}=\Vert X_{BA}\Vert _{(B:q,A:p)}\le \Vert X_{AB}\Vert _{(A:p,B:q)}=\Vert X\Vert _{(A:p,B:q)}$$\end{document}‖X‖(B:q,A:p)=‖XBA‖(B:q,A:p)≤‖XAB‖(A:p,B:q)=‖X‖(A:p,B:q), whenever \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q\ge p$$\end{document}q≥p. Further due to (6) it holds that for any operator space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X}$$\end{document}X and quantum systems E, A, B, 8\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert {{\,\textrm{id}\,}}_E\otimes F_{A\leftrightarrow B}\otimes {{\,\textrm{id}\,}}_{\mathcal {X}}\Vert _{(E:t,A:q,B:q;\mathcal {X})\rightarrow (E:t,B:q,A:q;\mathcal {X})} = 1 \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖idE⊗FA↔B⊗idX‖(E:t,A:q,B:q;X)→(E:t,B:q,A:q;X)=1is a complete isometry for any q.

The CB norm as defined above satisfies nice properties, which makes it very versatile and powerful. One of them is a very simple general chain rule, which we make use of multiple times.

Lemma 2.8

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi :\mathcal {X}\rightarrow \mathcal {Y}$$\end{document}Ψ:X→Y and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi :\mathcal {Y}\rightarrow \mathcal {Z}$$\end{document}Φ:Y→Z be two maps between arbitary operator spaces \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X},\mathcal {Y},\mathcal {Z}$$\end{document}X,Y,Z, then\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert \Phi \circ \Psi \Vert _{cb,\mathcal {X}\rightarrow \mathcal {Z}} \le \Vert \Psi \Vert _{cb,\mathcal {X}\rightarrow \mathcal {Y}}\cdot \Vert \Phi \Vert _{cb,\mathcal {Y}\rightarrow \mathcal {Z}} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖Φ∘Ψ‖cb,X→Z≤‖Ψ‖cb,X→Y·‖Φ‖cb,Y→Z

Rényi conditional entropies

We emphasize an important consequence of the variational expression that makes it a powerful tool in quantum information theory. Namely that by the first variational expression in Theorem 2.5 one may express the optimized Rényi-conditional entropy as a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert \cdot \Vert _{(1,\alpha )}$$\end{document}‖·‖(1,α) norm. Recall that for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1< \alpha <\infty $$\end{document}1<α<∞ the sandwiched Rényi\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$-\alpha $$\end{document}-α divergence [19, \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho ,\sigma \in \mathcal {D}(\mathcal {H})$$\end{document}ρ,σ∈D(H) is defined as\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} D_\alpha (\rho \Vert \sigma ):= \frac{\alpha }{\alpha -1}\log \Vert \sigma ^{\frac{1-\alpha }{2\alpha }}\rho \sigma ^{\frac{1-\alpha }{2\alpha }}\Vert _\alpha \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Dα(ρ‖σ):=αα-1log‖σ1-α2αρσ1-α2α‖αand the optimized Rényi\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} standard conditional entropy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs}
