of the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{id}\,}}_Q$$\end{document}idQ on the left is due to the definition of the CB norm. For the other inequality, for some system \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_1, E_2$$\end{document}E1,E2, let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X_{E_1Q} \in {{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {S}}\,}}_p[{{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {H}}\,}}_{E_1}, {{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {S}}\,}}_q({{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {H}}\,}}_{Q})]$$\end{document}XE1Q∈Sp[HE1,Sq(HQ)] and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Y_{E_2 {{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {X}}\,}}} \in {{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {S}}\,}}_{p}[{{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {H}}\,}}_{E_2}, {{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {X}}\,}}]$$\end{document}YE2X∈Sp[HE2,X].

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E=E_1E_2$$\end{document}E=E1E2, then\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert \Phi \otimes \Psi \Vert _{cb,S_q[\mathcal {H}_Q,\mathcal {X}]\rightarrow S_p[\mathcal {H}_P,\mathcal {Y}]}&\ge \Vert ({{\,\textrm{id}\,}}_{E_1}\otimes {{\,\textrm{id}\,}}_{E_2}\otimes \Phi \otimes \Psi )(X_{E_1Q}\otimes Y_{E_2\mathcal {X}})\Vert _{(E_1:p,E_2:p,P:p;\mathcal {Y})} \\&= \Vert ({{\,\textrm{id}\,}}_{E_1}\otimes \Phi )(X_{E_1Q})\otimes ({{\,\textrm{id}\,}}_{E_2}\otimes \Psi )( Y_{E_2\mathcal {X}})\Vert _{(E_1:p,P:p,E_2:p;\mathcal {Y})} \\ &= \Vert ({{\,\textrm{id}\,}}_{E_1}\otimes \Phi )(X_{EQ})\Vert _{(E_1:p,P:p)}\cdot \Vert ({{\,\textrm{id}\,}}_{E_2}\otimes \Psi )( Y_{E_2\mathcal {X}})\Vert _{(E_2:p;\mathcal {Y})}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖Φ⊗Ψ‖cb,Sq[HQ,X]→Sp[HP,Y]≥‖(idE1⊗idE2⊗Φ⊗Ψ)(XE1Q⊗YE2X)‖(E1:p,E2:p,P:p;Y)=‖(idE1⊗Φ)(XE1Q)⊗(idE2⊗Ψ)(YE2X)‖(E1:p,P:p,E2:p;Y)=‖(idE1⊗Φ)(XEQ)‖(E1:p,P:p)·‖(idE2⊗Ψ)(YE2X)‖(E2:p;Y),where the last equality follows from Propostion 2.7 applied to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_1P,E_2\mathcal {Y}$$\end{document}E1P,E2Y. Now taking the supremum over X, Y and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_1,E_2$$\end{document}E1,E2 yields the claim.

The multiplicativity result for n tensored CP maps follows now directly via induction. For simplicity denote with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q^n_j:=Q_j...Q_n$$\end{document}Qjn:=Qj...Qn and with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q_j^n:=(q_j,...,q_n)$$\end{document}qjn:=(qj,...,qn) and similarly for P, p. Now the above is the induction start and the step follows via\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \bigg \Vert \bigotimes _{i=1}^n\Phi _i\bigg \Vert _{cb,(Q^n:q^n)\rightarrow (P^n:p^n)}&= \bigg \Vert \Phi _1\otimes \bigotimes _{i=2}^n\Phi _i\bigg \Vert _{cb,(Q_1:q_1,Q_2^n:q_2^n)\rightarrow (P_1:p_1,P_2^n:p_2^n)} \\&=\Vert \Phi _i\Vert _{cb,(Q_1:q_1)\rightarrow (P_1:p_1)}\cdot \bigg \Vert \bigotimes _{i=2}^n\Phi _i\bigg \Vert _{cb,(Q_2^n:q_2^n)\rightarrow (P_2^n:p_2^n)}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖⨂i=1nΦi‖cb,(Qn:qn)→(Pn:pn)=‖Φ1⊗⨂i=2nΦi‖cb,(Q1:q1,Q2n:q2n)→(P1:p1,P2n:p2n)=‖Φi‖cb,(Q1:q1)→(P1:p1)·‖⨂i=2nΦi‖cb,(Q2n:q2n)→(P2n:p2n),where in the second line we used the above with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X}=\mathcal {S}_{q_2}[...\mathcal {S}_{q_n}(\mathcal {H}_{Q_n})...]$$\end{document}X=Sq2[...Sqn(HQn)...] and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {Y}=\mathcal {S}_{p_2}[...\mathcal {S}_{p_n}(\mathcal {H}_{P_n})...]$$\end{document}Y=Sp2[...Spn(HPn)...]. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

As a direct consequence, we get a special case of the generalized EAT chain rule [17] for product quantum channels.

Corollary 4.9

(A chain rule under product maps). Consider a CP map of product form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi _{Q_1Q_2 \rightarrow RS} = \phi _{Q_1 \rightarrow R} \otimes \psi _{Q_2 \rightarrow S}$$\end{document}ΦQ1Q2→RS=ϕQ1→R⊗ψQ2→S. Then we have10\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert \Phi \otimes {{\,\textrm{id}\,}}_T\Vert _{(Q_1:1,T:\alpha ,Q_2:1)\rightarrow (R:1,S:\alpha ,T:\alpha )} \le \Vert \Phi \Vert _{cb,(Q_1:1,Q_2:1)\rightarrow (R:1,S:\alpha )} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖Φ⊗idT‖(Q1:1,T:α,Q2:1)→(R:1,S:α,T:α)≤‖Φ‖cb,(Q1:1,Q2:1)→(R:1,S:α)which implies11\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} H^{\uparrow }_\alpha (ST|R)_{(\Phi \otimes {{\,\textrm{id}\,}}_T)(\rho _{Q_{1}Q_{2}T})} \ge H^{\uparrow }_\alpha (T|Q_1)_{\rho } + \inf _{\sigma \in \mathcal {D}({{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {H}}\,}}_{Q_1Q_2} \otimes {{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {H}}\,}}_{\tilde{Q}})} H^{\uparrow }_\alpha (S|R \tilde{Q})_{(\Phi \otimes {{\,\textrm{id}\,}}_{\tilde{Q}})(\sigma _{Q_1Q_2\tilde{Q}})}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Hα↑(ST|R)(Φ⊗idT)(ρQ1Q2T)≥Hα↑(T|Q1)ρ+infσ∈D(HQ1Q2⊗HQ~)Hα↑(S|RQ~)(Φ⊗idQ~)(σQ1Q2Q~),where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{Q}$$\end{document}Q~ is a purifying system isomorphic to Q.

Remark 4.10

This chain rule (11) is similar to the one in [17, Lemma 3.6] with the replacements \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q_1 \rightarrow E$$\end{document}Q1→E, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q_2 \rightarrow R$$\end{document}Q2→R, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S \rightarrow A'$$\end{document}S→A′, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T \rightarrow A$$\end{document}T→A, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R \rightarrow E'$$\end{document}R→E′. The differences are the we assume the product condition which is stronger than the non-signalling condition in [17, Lemma 3.6], but we use \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_{\alpha }^{\uparrow }$$\end{document}Hα↑ instead of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_{\alpha }$$\end{document}Hα and our chain rule is applicable to any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \ge 1$$\end{document}α≥1 and there is no loss in the parameter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha $$\end{document}α.

Proof

We first establish (10). By the fact that we can combine systems with the same parameter (Proposition 2.6) and that the completely bounded norm is multiplicative (Theorem 4.7), we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\Vert \Phi \otimes {{\,\textrm{id}\,}}_{T} \Vert _{(Q_1:1,T:\alpha ,Q_2:1)\rightarrow (R:1,S:\alpha ,T:\alpha )} \\&\quad = \Vert \phi _{Q_1\rightarrow R}\otimes {{\,\textrm{id}\,}}_T\otimes \psi _{Q_2\rightarrow S}\Vert _{(Q_1:1,T:\alpha ,Q_2:1)\rightarrow (R:1,S:\alpha ,T:\alpha )} \\&\quad = \Vert \phi _{Q_1\rightarrow R}\otimes {{\,\textrm{id}\,}}_T\otimes \psi _{Q_2\rightarrow S}\Vert _{(Q_1:1,T:\alpha ,Q_2:1)\rightarrow (R:1,T:\alpha ,S:\alpha )} \\&\quad \le \Vert \phi _{Q_1\rightarrow R}\otimes {{\,\textrm{id}\,}}_T\otimes \psi _{Q_2\rightarrow S}\Vert _{cb,(Q_1:1,T:\alpha ,Q_2:1)\rightarrow (R:1,T:\alpha ,S:\alpha )} \\&\quad = \Vert \phi \Vert _{cb,(Q_1:1) \rightarrow (R:1)} \Vert {{\,\textrm{id}\,}}_T\Vert _{cb,T:\alpha \rightarrow T:\alpha } \Vert \psi _{Q_2\rightarrow S}\Vert _{cb,(Q_2:1) \rightarrow (S:\alpha )} \\&\quad = \Vert \phi \otimes \psi \Vert _{cb,(Q_1:1,Q_2:1) \rightarrow (R:1,S:\alpha )} \\&\quad = \Vert \Phi \Vert _{cb,(Q_1:1,Q_2:1) \rightarrow (R:1,S:\alpha )} = \Vert \Phi \Vert _{cb,(Q_1Q_2:1) \rightarrow (R:1,S:\alpha )}^+\,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖Φ⊗idT‖(Q1:1,T:α,Q2:1)→(R:1,S:α,T:α)=‖ϕQ1→R⊗idT⊗ψQ2→S‖(Q1:1,T:α,Q2:1)→(R:1,S:α,T:α)=‖ϕQ1→R⊗idT⊗ψQ2→S‖(Q1:1,T:α,Q2:1)→(R:1,T:α,S:α)≤‖ϕQ1→R⊗idT⊗ψQ2→S‖cb,(Q1:1,T:α,Q2:1)→(R:1,T:α,S:α)=‖ϕ‖cb,(Q1:1)→(R:1)‖idT‖cb,T:α→T:α‖ψQ2→S‖cb,(Q2:1)→(S:α)=‖ϕ⊗ψ‖cb,(Q1:1,Q2:1)→(R:1,S:α)=‖Φ‖cb,(Q1:1,Q2:1)→(R:1,S:α)=‖Φ‖cb,(Q1Q2:1)→(R:1,S:α)+.We now show how to deduce the chain rule (11). As before, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{\alpha }{1-\alpha } \log \Vert \Phi \Vert _{cb,(Q_1:1,Q_2:1) \rightarrow (R:1,S:\alpha )} = \inf _{\sigma \in \mathcal {D}({{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {H}}\,}}_{Q_1Q_2} \otimes {{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {H}}\,}}_{\tilde{Q}})} H_{\alpha }^{\uparrow }(S|R\tilde{Q})$$\end{document}α1-αlog‖Φ‖cb,(Q1:1,Q2:1)→(R:1,S:α)=infσ∈D(HQ1Q2⊗HQ~)Hα↑(S|RQ~). Moreover, given a positive semidefinite matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho _{Q_1Q_2T}$$\end{document}ρQ1Q2T, we have that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_{\alpha }^{\uparrow }(T|Q_1)_{\rho } = \frac{\alpha }{1-\alpha }$$\end{document}Hα↑(T|Q1)ρ=α1-α \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \log \Vert \rho \Vert _{(Q_1:1,T:\alpha , Q_2:1)}$$\end{document}log‖ρ‖(Q1:1,T:α,Q2:1) because \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert \rho \Vert _{(Q_1:1,T:\alpha , Q_2:1)} = \Vert \hbox {tr}_{Q_2} \rho _{Q_1TQ_2} \Vert _{(Q_1:1,T:\alpha )}$$\end{document}‖ρ‖(Q1:1,T:α,Q2:1)=‖trQ2ρQ1TQ2‖(Q1:1,T:α) as proved in [10, Section 3.5]. Furthermore, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_{\alpha }^{\uparrow }(ST|R)_{(\Phi \otimes {{\,\textrm{id}\,}}_T)(\rho )} = \frac{\alpha }{1-\alpha } \log \Vert (\Phi \otimes {{\,\textrm{id}\,}}_T)(\rho )\Vert _{(R:1,S:\alpha ,T:\alpha )}$$\end{document}Hα↑(ST|R)(Φ⊗idT)(ρ)=α1-αlog‖(Φ⊗idT)(ρ)‖(R:1,S:α,T:α). \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Motivated by applications in Sect. 5, we now consider multiplicativity with a different order. The maps \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi $$\end{document}Φ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi $$\end{document}Ψ have composite output systems \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_1S_1$$\end{document}R1S1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_2S_2$$\end{document}R2S2 and the relevant norm on the output is a multi-index Schatten norm in the order \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_1R_2S_1S_2$$\end{document}R1R2S1S2. We also restrict ourselves to an index q for the R systems (the index of the input systems Q) and an index p for the S systems.

Theorem 4.11

(Multiplicativity of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q \rightarrow (q,p)-$$\end{document}q→(q,p)-CB-norms). Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R^n:=R_1...R_n, S^n:=S_1...S_n$$\end{document}Rn:=R1...Rn,Sn:=S1...Sn.

Remark 4.12

Note firstly that this result is also a generalization of [10, Theorem 11 and Theorem 13 c)] but in a different way: we recover the upper bounds of [10, Theorem 11 and Theorem 13 c)] by letting the systems R be trivial. Secondly, note that due to Lemma A.2 and Corollary 4.5 the optimization in the CB norms on both sides can be restricted to positive states only.

Proof of Theorem 4.11

Note that we may assume that all the norms are finite, otherwise the equality clearly holds. We apply Lemma 2.8 and write \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi \otimes \Psi $$\end{document}Φ⊗Ψ as a composition of four maps, since the order in the multi-index norm we are considering does not respect the tensor product structure of the maps: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi \otimes \Psi .$$\end{document}Φ⊗Ψ. We write it as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(F_{R_1\leftrightarrow R_2}\otimes {{\,\textrm{id}\,}}_{S_1S_2})\circ ({{\,\textrm{id}\,}}_{R_2} \otimes \Phi \otimes {{\,\textrm{id}\,}}_{S_2}) \circ (F_{Q_1 \leftrightarrow R_2} \otimes {{\,\textrm{id}\,}}_{S_2}) \circ ({{\,\textrm{id}\,}}_{Q_1} \otimes \Psi )$$\end{document}(FR1↔R2⊗idS1S2)∘(idR2⊗Φ⊗idS2)∘(FQ1↔R2⊗idS2)∘(idQ1⊗Ψ). Note that we wrote the swap explicitly to emphasize the order in the multi-index Schatten norms that we use. For additional clarity, we specify the operator spaces for each map: The input operator space for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({{\,\textrm{id}\,}}_{Q_1} \otimes \Psi )$$\end{document}(idQ1⊗Ψ) is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {X}}\,}}_1 = {{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {S}}\,}}_q[{{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {H}}\,}}_{Q_1},{{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {S}}\,}}_q({{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {H}}\,}}_{Q_2})]$$\end{document}X1=Sq[HQ1,Sq(HQ2)] and the output is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {X}}\,}}_2 = {{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {S}}\,}}_q[{{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {H}}\,}}_{Q_1}, {{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {S}}\,}}_{q}[{{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {H}}\,}}_{R_2}, {{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {S}}\,}}_{p}({{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {H}}\,}}_{S_2})]]$$\end{document}X2=Sq[HQ1,Sq[HR2,Sp(HS2)]]. The output operator space of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(F_{Q_1 \leftrightarrow R_2} \otimes {{\,\textrm{id}\,}}_{S_2})$$\end{document}(FQ1↔R2⊗idS2) is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {X}}\,}}_3 = {{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {S}}\,}}_q[{{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {H}}\,}}_{R_2}, {{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {S}}\,}}_{q}[{{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {H}}\,}}_{Q_1}, {{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {S}}\,}}_{p}({{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {H}}\,}}_{S_2})]]$$\end{document}X3=Sq[HR2,Sq[HQ1,Sp(HS2)]] and the output operator space of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({{\,\textrm{id}\,}}_{R_2} \otimes \Phi \otimes {{\,\textrm{id}\,}}_{S_2})$$\end{document}(idR2⊗Φ⊗idS2) is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {X}}\,}}_4 = {{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {S}}\,}}_q[{{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {H}}\,}}_{R_2}, {{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {S}}\,}}_{q}[{{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {H}}\,}}_{R_1}, {{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {S}}\,}}_{p}[{{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {H}}\,}}_{S_1}, {{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {S}}\,}}_{p}({{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {H}}\,}}_{S_2})]]]$$\end{document}X4=Sq[HR2,Sq[HR1,Sp[HS1,Sp(HS2)]]]. The last map now maps \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X}_4$$\end{document}X4 into \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X}_5:={{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {S}}\,}}_q[{{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {H}}\,}}_{R_1}, {{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {S}}\,}}_{q}[{{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {H}}\,}}_{R_2}, {{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {S}}\,}}_{p}[{{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {H}}\,}}_{S_1}, {{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {S}}\,}}_{p}({{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {H}}\,}}_{S_2})]]]$$\end{document}X5:=Sq[HR1,Sq[HR2,Sp[HS1,Sp(HS2)]]]. It now remains to bound the CB norm of each one of these three maps. First, by definition of the CB norm, we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert ({{\,\textrm{id}\,}}_{Q_1} \otimes \Psi ) \Vert _{cb,{{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {X}}\,}}_1 \rightarrow {{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {X}}\,}}_2} = \Vert \Psi \Vert _{cb, {{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {S}}\,}}_q({{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {H}}\,}}_{Q_2}) \rightarrow {{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {S}}\,}}_{q}[{{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {H}}\,}}_{R_2}, {{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {S}}\,}}_{p}({{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {H}}\,}}_{S_2})]}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖(idQ1⊗Ψ)‖cb,X1→X2=‖Ψ‖cb,Sq(HQ2)→Sq[HR2,Sp(HS2)].Second, using the fact that the swap between systems of equal Schatten indices is a complete isometry and its CB norm is not affected by an identity on the right (8) gives\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert F_{Q_1 \leftrightarrow R_2} \otimes {{\,\textrm{id}\,}}_{S_2} \Vert _{cb, {{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {X}}\,}}_2 \rightarrow {{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {X}}\,}}_3}&= 1, \\ \Vert F_{R_1\leftrightarrow R_2}\otimes {{\,\textrm{id}\,}}_{S_1S_2}\Vert _{cb,\mathcal {X}_4\rightarrow \mathcal {X}_5}&= 1. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖FQ1↔R2⊗idS2‖cb,X2→X3=1,‖FR1↔R2⊗idS1S2‖cb,X4→X5=1.Third, using Corollary 4.5 and also the definition of the completely bounded norm\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert {{\,\textrm{id}\,}}_{R_2} \otimes \Phi \otimes {{\,\textrm{id}\,}}_{S_2} \Vert _{cb,{{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {X}}\,}}_3 \rightarrow {{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {X}}\,}}_4} = \Vert \Phi \Vert _{cb,{{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {S}}\,}}_q({{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {H}}\,}}_{Q_1}) \rightarrow {{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {S}}\,}}_{q}[{{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {H}}\,}}_{R_1},{{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {S}}\,}}_p({{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {H}}\,}}_{S_1})]}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖idR2⊗Φ⊗idS2‖cb,X3→X4=‖Φ‖cb,Sq(HQ1)→Sq[HR1,Sp(HS1)].Finally, we get12\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\Vert \Phi \otimes \Psi \Vert _{cb,(Q_1:q,Q_2:q)\rightarrow (R_1:q,R_2:q,S_1:p,S_2:p)} \nonumber \\&\quad \le \Vert \Phi \Vert _{cb,(Q_1:q)\rightarrow (R_1:q,S_1:p)} \cdot \Vert \Psi \Vert _{cb,(Q_2:q)\rightarrow (R_2:q,S_2:p)}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖Φ⊗Ψ‖cb,(Q1:q,Q2:q)→(R1:q,R2:q,S1:p,S2:p)≤‖Φ‖cb,(Q1:q)→(R1:q,S1:p)·‖Ψ‖cb,(Q2:q)→(R2:q,S2:p).The proof of the n-fold statement follows similarly to in Theorem 4.7 by induction over the multiplicativity statement. Denote with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q^j:=Q_1...Q_j$$\end{document}Qj:=Q1...Qj for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1\le j\le n$$\end{document}1≤j≤n, and analogously \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R^j,S^j$$\end{document}Rj,Sj. The theorem is the induction start, for the induction step observe\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \bigg \Vert \bigotimes ^n_{i=1}\Phi _i\bigg \Vert _{cb,(Q:q)\rightarrow (R:q,S:p)}&= \left\| \bigotimes ^{n-1}_{i=1}\Phi _i\otimes \Phi _n\right\| _{cb,({Q^{n-1}:q},{Q_n}:q)\rightarrow (R^{n-1}:q,{R_n}:q,S^{n-1}:p,S_n:p)} \\ &\le \bigg \Vert \bigotimes ^{n-1}_{i=1}\Phi _i\bigg \Vert _{cb,(Q^{n-1}:q)\rightarrow (R^{n-1}:q,S^{n-1}:p)}\\&\cdot \big \Vert \Phi _n\big \Vert _{cb,(Q_n:q)\rightarrow (R_n:q,S_n:p)}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖⨂i=1nΦi‖cb,(Q:q)→(R:q,S:p)=⨂i=1n-1Φi⊗Φncb,(Qn-1:q,Qn:q)→(Rn-1:q,Rn:q,Sn-1:p,Sn:p)≤‖⨂i=1n-1Φi‖cb,(Qn-1:q)→(Rn-1:q,Sn-1:p)·‖Φn‖cb,(Qn:q)→(Rn:q,Sn:p),where the inequality in the second line follows from (12). \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

We also obtain a submultiplicativity result for the sequential composition of CP maps.

Theorem 4.13

(Sequential composition of maps). Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi :Q_1\rightarrow R_1Q_2S_1, \Psi :Q_2\rightarrow R_2S_2$$\end{document}Φ:Q1→R1Q2S1,Ψ:Q2→R2S2 be two CP maps and write \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R^2:=R_1R_2$$\end{document}R2:=R1R2, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S^2:=S_1S_2$$\end{document}S2:=S1S2, then for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1\le q,r,s\le \infty $$\end{document}1≤q,r,s≤∞\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert \Psi \circ \Phi \Vert _{cb,(Q_1:q)\rightarrow (R^2:r,S^2:s)}&\le \Vert \Psi \Vert ^{+}_{cb,(Q_2:q)\rightarrow (R_2:r,S_2:s)} \cdot \Vert \Phi \Vert ^{+}_{cb,(Q_1:q)\rightarrow (R_1:r,Q_2:q,S_1:s)}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖Ψ∘Φ‖cb,(Q1:q)→(R2:r,S2:s)≤‖Ψ‖cb,(Q2:q)→(R2:r,S2:s)+·‖Φ‖cb,(Q1:q)→(R1:r,Q2:q,S1:s)+.

Proof

Using the fact that consecutive systems with the same Schatten index can be swapped (Proposition 2.6), we can write \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert \Psi \circ \Phi \Vert _{cb,(Q_1:q)\rightarrow (R^2:r,S^2:s)} = \Vert \Psi \circ \Phi \Vert _{cb,(Q_1:q)\rightarrow (R_1:r,R_2:r,S_2:s,S_1:s)}$$\end{document}‖Ψ∘Φ‖cb,(Q1:q)→(R2:r,S2:s)=‖Ψ∘Φ‖cb,(Q1:q)→(R1:r,R2:r,S2:s,S1:s). We then write the composition more explicitly including identities: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi \circ \Phi = ({{\,\textrm{id}\,}}_{R_1} \otimes \Psi \otimes {{\,\textrm{id}\,}}_{S_1}) \circ \Phi $$\end{document}Ψ∘Φ=(idR1⊗Ψ⊗idS1)∘Φ and then use Lemma 2.8 to get\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\Vert \Psi \circ \Phi \Vert _{cb,(Q_1:q)\rightarrow (R_1:r,R_2:r,S^2:s,S_1:s)} \\&\le \Vert {{\,\textrm{id}\,}}_{R_1} \otimes \Psi \otimes {{\,\textrm{id}\,}}_{S_1} \Vert _{cb,(R_1:r,Q_2:q,S_1:s) \rightarrow (R_1:r,R_2:r,S_2:s,S_1:s)} \Vert \Phi \Vert _{cb,(Q_1:q) \rightarrow (R_1:r,Q_2:q,S_1:s)}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖Ψ∘Φ‖cb,(Q1:q)→(R1:r,R2:r,S2:s,S1:s)≤‖idR1⊗Ψ⊗idS1‖cb,(R1:r,Q2:q,S1:s)→(R1:r,R2:r,S2:s,S1:s)‖Φ‖cb,(Q1:q)→(R1:r,Q2:q,S1:s).Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi $$\end{document}Φ is CP, by Lemma A.2 we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert \Phi \Vert _{cb,(Q_1:q) \rightarrow (R_1:r,Q_2:q,S_1:s)} = \Vert \Phi \Vert _{cb,(Q_1:q) \rightarrow (R_1:r,Q_2:q,S_1:s)}^+$$\end{document}‖Φ‖cb,(Q1:q)→(R1:r,Q2:q,S1:s)=‖Φ‖cb,(Q1:q)→(R1:r,Q2:q,S1:s)+. Then by definition of the CB norm and then using Corollary 4.5, we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\Vert {{\,\textrm{id}\,}}_{R_1} \otimes \Psi \otimes {{\,\textrm{id}\,}}_{S_1} \Vert _{cb,(R_1:r,Q_2:q,S_1:s) \rightarrow (R_1:r,R_2:r,S_2:s,S_1:s)}\\&= \Vert \Psi \otimes {{\,\textrm{id}\,}}_{S_1} \Vert _{cb,(Q_2:q,S_1:s) \rightarrow (R_2:r,S_2:s,S_1:s)} \\&= \Vert \Psi \Vert ^+_{cb,(Q_2:q) \rightarrow (R_2:r,S_2:s)}\,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖idR1⊗Ψ⊗idS1‖cb,(R1:r,Q2:q,S1:s)→(R1:r,R2:r,S2:s,S1:s)=‖Ψ⊗idS1‖cb,(Q2:q,S1:s)→(R2:r,S2:s,S1:s)=‖Ψ‖cb,(Q2:q)→(R2:r,S2:s)+,which proves the desired result. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Linear constraint setting and additivity

For the applications in quantum cryptography that we present in the next section, it is important to be able to restrict the optimization with a linear constraint. So we consider Theorem 4.11 for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q = 1$$\end{document}q=1 by restricting the optimization implicit in the CB-norms to states satisfying some linear constraints. To enforce the linear constraints, we introduce the following definitions.

Definition 4.14

(Restricted state spaces). Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}_Q$$\end{document}HQ be the Hilbert space of a quantum system. Then we specify a linear constraint on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {D}(\mathcal {H}_Q)$$\end{document}D(HQ) by a linear CPTP map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {N}:Q\rightarrow Q^\prime $$\end{document}N:Q→Q′ and a state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau \in \mathcal {D}(\mathcal {H}_{Q^\prime })$$\end{document}τ∈D(HQ′), where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q^\prime $$\end{document}Q′ is some other quantum system. Given such a tuple \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r=(\mathcal {N},\tau )$$\end{document}r=(N,τ) we define\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {D}_r({{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {H}}\,}}_Q):= \{\rho \in {{\,\textrm{Pos}\,}}(Q)|\mathcal {N}(\rho ) = \tau \hbox {Tr}[\rho ]\}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Dr(HQ):={ρ∈Pos(Q)|N(ρ)=τTr[ρ]}.More generally, let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{\mathcal {D}_{r_i}({{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {H}}\,}}_{Q_i})\}_{i\le n}$$\end{document}{Dri(HQi)}i≤n be n such restricted sets of states defined in terms of the tuples \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r^n=\{r_i\}_{i=1}^n=\{(\mathcal {N}_i,\tau _i)\}^n_{i=1}$$\end{document}rn={ri}i=1n={(Ni,τi)}i=1n and E any other quantum system. Then we define13\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {D}^E_{r^n}({{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {H}}\,}}_{Q^n})= \left\{ \rho \in {{\,\textrm{Pos}\,}}(EQ^n)|\textstyle \left( \bigotimes _{i\le n}\mathcal {N}_i\right) \circ \hbox {tr}_E[\rho ] = \textstyle \bigotimes _{i\le n}\tau _i\hbox {Tr}[\rho ]\right\} . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}DnE(HQn)=ρ∈Pos(EQn)|⨂i≤nNi∘trE[ρ]=⨂i≤nτiTr[ρ].

In particular, when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {N}:Q\rightarrow \mathbb {C}, \rho \mapsto \hbox {Tr}[\rho ]$$\end{document}N:Q→C,ρ↦Tr[ρ] is the full trace and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau = 1$$\end{document}τ=1, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {D}_r({{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {H}}\,}}_Q) = \mathcal {D}({{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {H}}\,}}_Q)$$\end{document}Dr(HQ)=D(HQ) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {D}^E_r({{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {H}}\,}}_Q) = \mathcal {D}({{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {H}}\,}}_{EQ})$$\end{document}DrE(HQ)=D(HEQ) is just the set of unrestricted normalized states. The restricted CB-norms of quantum channels are then naturally defined as follows.

Definition 4.15

(Restricted CB-norms). Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r:=(\mathcal {N},\tau )$$\end{document}r:=(N,τ) define a linear restriction and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi :Q\rightarrow RS$$\end{document}Φ:Q→RS be a CP map, then we define the restricted completely bounded norm of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi $$\end{document}Φ as\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert \Phi \Vert _{r,cb,(Q:q)\rightarrow (R:r,S:s)}:=\sup _E\sup _{\rho \in \mathcal {D}_r^E({{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {H}}\,}}_Q)}\frac{\Vert ({{\,\textrm{id}\,}}_E\otimes \Phi )(\rho )\Vert _{(E:q,R:r,S:s)}}{\Vert \rho \Vert _{(E:q,Q:q)}}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖Φ‖r,cb,(Q:q)→(R:r,S:s):=supEsupρ∈DrE(HQ)‖(idE⊗Φ)(ρ)‖(E:q,R:r,S:s)‖ρ‖(E:q,Q:q).

Now, we may strengthen Theorem 4.11 to also hold under arbitrary linear constraints of the form (13).

Theorem 4.16

(Multiplicativity of restricted CB \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1\rightarrow (1,p)$$\end{document}1→(1,p) norms). Let n CP maps \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi _i:Q_i\rightarrow R_iS_i$$\end{document}Φi:Qi→RiSi such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert \Phi _i\Vert _{cb,(Q_i:1)\rightarrow (R_i:1,S_i:p)}<\infty $$\end{document}‖Φi‖cb,(Qi:1)→(Ri:1,Si:p)<∞ and n linear restrictions governed by triples \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r_i:=(Q_i^\prime ,\mathcal {N}_i,\tau _i)$$\end{document}ri:=(Qi′,Ni,τi) be given. Denote the combined spaces \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q^n:=Q_1...Q_n$$\end{document}Qn:=Q1...Qn, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R^n:=R_1...R_n$$\end{document}Rn:=R1...Rn, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S^n:=S_1...S_n$$\end{document}Sn:=S1...Sn and the combined linear restriction as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r^n:=(\otimes _{i=1}^n\mathcal {N}_i,\otimes _{i=1}^n\tau _i)$$\end{document}rn:=(⊗i=1nNi,⊗i=1nτi). Then it holds that14\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \bigg \Vert \bigotimes _{i=1}^n \Phi _i\bigg \Vert _{r^n,cb,(Q^n:1)\rightarrow (R^n:1,S^n:p)}&\le \prod ^n_{i=1} \Vert \Phi _i\Vert _{r_i,cb,(Q_i:1)\rightarrow (R_i:1,S_i:p)}\,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖⨂i=1nΦi‖rn,cb,(Qn:1)→(Rn:1,Sn:p)≤∏i=1n‖Φi‖ri,cb,(Qi:1)→(Ri:1,Si:p).

Remark 4.17

This is a generalization of a result from [25], in which this statement, formulated in terms of Rényi entropies and derived via very different tools, was shown to hold in the special case where all channels \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi _i=\mathcal {M}$$\end{document}Φi=M are equal.

Proof

The proof follows via lifting Theorem 4.11 to the restricted setting, in the same manner as was done in [25]. Hence it is repeated in Appendix B. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Applications to Quantum Cryptography

We now show how the multiplicativity of restricted CB \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1\rightarrow (1,p)$$\end{document}1→(1,p) norms shown in Theorem 4.16 can be used to prove the security of time-adaptive quantum cryptographic protocols. In this section we will assume all Hilbert spaces to be finite dimensional.

f-weighted Rényi entropy

A key quantity used in security proofs is the f-weighted Rényi entropy [25], which is defined as follows. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {X}$$\end{document}X be a finite set, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f:\mathbb {X}\rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}f:X→R a function, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho _{XAE} = \sum _x |{x}\rangle \langle {x}|_X \otimes \rho _{AE}^x $$\end{document}ρXAE=∑x|x⟩⟨x|X⊗ρAEx a state that is classical on system X, then for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha >1$$\end{document}α>1, define the f-weighted Renyi entropy as\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} H_\alpha ^{\uparrow ,f}(A|XE)_{\rho }&:= \frac{\alpha }{1-\alpha } \log \Vert 2^{\tfrac{\alpha -1}{\alpha }f_X} \cdot \rho _{EXA}\Vert _{(EX:1,A:\alpha )}\\&= \frac{\alpha }{1-\alpha } \log \sum _{x\in \mathbb {X}} 2^{\tfrac{\alpha -1}{\alpha }f(x)} \Vert \rho _{EA}^x\Vert _{(E:1,A:\alpha )} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Hα↑,f(A|XE)ρ:=α1-αlog‖2α-1αfX·ρEXA‖(EX:1,A:α)=α1-αlog∑x∈X2α-1αf(x)‖ρEAx‖(E:1,A:α)where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_X = \sum _x f(x) |{x}\rangle \langle {x}|$$\end{document}fX=∑xf(x)|x⟩⟨x| is a diagonal operator, which commutes with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho _{EXA}$$\end{document}ρEXA. In particular, we note that when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f(x)=0$$\end{document}f(x)=0 for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in \mathbb {X}$$\end{document}x∈X, we recover the usual Rényi entropy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_\alpha ^{\uparrow ,f}(A|XE)_{\rho } = H_\alpha ^{\uparrow }(A|XE)_{\rho }$$\end{document}Hα↑,f(A|XE)ρ=Hα↑(A|XE)ρ.

As we will see now, Theorem 4.16 implies a chain rule for f-weighted Rényi entropies. Assume we need to minimize the f-weighted Rényi entropy over a set of states of the form\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \{ \rho _{EX^nA^n} = ({{\,\textrm{id}\,}}_E\otimes \mathcal {M}^n) (\rho _{EQ^n}) \, | \, \rho _{EQ^n} \in \mathcal {D}_r^E({{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {H}}\,}}_{Q^n})\} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}{ρEXnAn=(idE⊗Mn)(ρEQn)|ρEQn∈DrE(HQn)}for all possible choices of environments E, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {M}^n = \bigotimes _{i=1}^n \mathcal {M}_i$$\end{document}Mn=⨂i=1nMi and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {M}_i:Q_i\rightarrow X_iA_i$$\end{document}Mi:Qi→XiAi are CP map and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {D}^E_{r^n}({{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {H}}\,}}_{Q^n})$$\end{document}DnE(HQn) is an environment embedded restricted state space as defined in (13). Assume moreover that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f^n(x^n)= \sum _i f_i(x_i)$$\end{document}fn(xn)=∑ifi(xi). Then the results below shows that the minimium is obtained for an tensor product state of the form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho _{{E^\prime }^nX^nA^n} = \bigotimes _{i=1}^n \rho _{E^\prime XA}^i$$\end{document}ρE′nXnAn=⨂i=1nρE′XAi with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho _{E^\prime XA}^i = {{\,\textrm{id}\,}}_{E^\prime }\otimes \mathcal {M}_i(\rho ^i_{E^\prime Q})$$\end{document}ρE′XAi=idE′⊗Mi(ρE′Qi) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho _{QE'}^i\in D_r^{E'}({{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {H}}\,}}_{Q_i})$$\end{document}ρQE′i∈Dr′(HQi) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E = {E'}^n$$\end{document}E=E′n, in which case then entropy is additive.

Corollary 5.1

(Reduction to independent attacks). For all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i\in [n]$$\end{document}i∈[n], let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {M}_i:Q_i\rightarrow X_iA_i$$\end{document}Mi:Qi→XiAi be a CP map between finite dimensional systems, with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X_i$$\end{document}Xi a classical register with basis elements labeled \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {X}$$\end{document}X, and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_i: \mathbb {X} \rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}fi:X→R. Set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q^n:=Q_1...Q_n, X^n:=X_1...X_n,$$\end{document}Qn:=Q1...Qn,Xn:=X1...Xn, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A^n:=A_1...A_n$$\end{document}An:=A1...An and define \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {M}^n = \bigotimes _{i=1}^n \mathcal {M}_i$$\end{document}Mn=⨂i=1nMi and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f^n(x^n) = \sum _{i=1}^n f_i(x_i)$$\end{document}fn(xn)=∑i=1nfi(xi). Then for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha >1$$\end{document}α>1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\inf _E\inf _{\rho \in \mathcal {D}^E_r({{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {H}}\,}}_{Q^n})} H_\alpha ^{\uparrow ,f_n}(A^n|X^nE)_{({{\,\textrm{id}\,}}_E\otimes \mathcal {M}^n)(\rho )}\\&= \sum _{i=1}^n \inf _E\inf _{\rho _i\in \mathcal {D}_r^E({{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {H}}\,}}_{Q_i})} H_\alpha ^{\uparrow ,f_i}(A_i|X_iE)_{({{\,\textrm{id}\,}}_E\otimes \mathcal {M}_i)(\rho _i)} \\ \end{aligned}$$\end{document}infEinfρ∈DrE(HQn)Hα↑,fn(An|XnE)(idE⊗Mn)(ρ)=∑i=1ninfEinfρi∈DrE(HQi)Hα↑,fi(Ai|XiE)(idE⊗Mi)(ρi)

Proof

Define the operators \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_{i,X_i}:= \sum _{x \in \mathbb {X}} f_i(x) |{x}\rangle $$\end{document}fi,Xi:=∑x∈Xfi(x)|x⟩ \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle {x}|_{X_i}$$\end{document}⟨x|Xi and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f^n_{X^n} = \sum _{x^n\in \mathbb {X}^n} f^n(x^n)|{x}\rangle \langle {x}|^{n}_{X^n}$$\end{document}fnn=∑xn∈Xnfn(xn)|x⟩⟨x|nn and the maps \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi _i: Q_i \rightarrow X_i A_i $$\end{document}Φi:Qi→XiAi defined by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi _i(\rho ):= 2^{\tfrac{\alpha -1}{2\alpha }f_{i,X_i}} \mathcal {M}_i(\rho ) 2^{\tfrac{\alpha -1}{2\alpha }f_{i,X_i}}$$\end{document}Φi(ρ):=2α-12αfi,XiMi(ρ)2α-12αfi,Xi, which are CP by construction, since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2^{\frac{\alpha -1}{2\alpha }f_{i,X_i}}$$\end{document}2α-12αfi,Xi are self-adjoint. Then, we can use Theorem 4.16 for the maps \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi _i$$\end{document}Φi with the replacements \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A_i \rightarrow S_i$$\end{document}Ai→Si and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X_i \rightarrow R_i$$\end{document}Xi→Ri and by applying \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{\alpha }{1-\alpha }\log $$\end{document}α1-αlog to each side of the equation (14) and noting that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bigotimes _{i=1}^n 2^{f_{i,X_i}} = 2^{f^n_{X^n}}$$\end{document}⨂i=1n2fi,Xi=2fnn we get that the LHS in upper bounded by the RHS. Equality follows from additivity of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^\uparrow _\alpha $$\end{document}Hα↑ under tensor products of states [24, Corollary 5.9]. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Definition of the protocol

For simplification, we will consider a random number generation (QRNG) protocol, which are closely related to quantum key distribution (QKD) protocols. Using standard techniques, the present security proof can be easily generalized to QKD [23, 25].

An n-round device-dependent random number generation protocol consists of two steps. First, we generate the raw data and then determine how much secure randomness can be extracted from that data. The first step can be represented as a CPTP map\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {M}^n: Q^n \rightarrow X^nA^n \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Mn:Qn→XnAnfrom an input space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q^n=Q_1 \dots Q_n$$\end{document}Qn=Q1⋯Qn to the output systems \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X^n$$\end{document}Xn and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A^n$$\end{document}An, representing respectively the public announcements and the raw key. Since they are classical variables, we can write operators on the corresponding Hilbert spaces as diagonal operators in some canonical basis, the elements of which are labeled by the finite sets \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {X},\mathbb {A}$$\end{document}X,A.

Independent rounds We assume that our protocol varies over times, but that different rounds of the protocol, corresponding to the CPTP maps \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {M}_t: {{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {D}}\,}}({{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {H}}\,}}_{Q})\rightarrow {{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {D}}\,}}({{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {H}}\,}}_{XA})$$\end{document}Mt:D(HQ)→D(HXA), act independently on different inputs, i.e.\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {M}^n = \bigotimes _{t=1}^n \mathcal {M}_t\,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Mn=⨂t=1nMt.Linear constraint on the input We assume that the protocol is applied to an initial unknown input state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho _{EQ^n}$$\end{document}ρEQn entangled with an arbitrary reference system E, and where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho _{Q^n} = \hbox {Tr}_E[\rho _{EQ^n}]$$\end{document}ρQn=TrE[ρEQn] satisfies a linear constraint of the form15\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \bigotimes _t \mathcal {N}_t(\rho _{Q^n}) = \bigotimes _t \tau _t \end{aligned}$$\end{document}⨂tNt(ρQn)=⨂tτtwhere \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {N}}\,}}_t: {{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {D}}\,}}({{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {H}}\,}}_Q)\rightarrow {{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {D}}\,}}({{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {H}}\,}}_{Q'})$$\end{document}Nt:D(HQ)→D(HQ′) is a completely positive map to some system \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q'$$\end{document}Q′ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau _t\ge 0$$\end{document}τt≥0 a positive semidefinite operator on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q'$$\end{document}Q′. This last condition is used in prepare-and-measure quantum key distribution protocols [28]. This corresponds to saying that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho _{EQ^n}\in \mathcal {D}^E_{r^n}({{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {H}}\,}}_{Q^n})$$\end{document}ρEQn∈DnE(HQn) for some restricted state space defined in (13).

Security and rate of the QRNG protocol

Post-processing To complete the protocol, we need to specify how we determine the amount of randomness that can be extracted from the raw key registers \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A^n$$\end{document}An. This is done using some function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g_n:\mathbb {X}^n\rightarrow \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}gn:Xn→N, which will be constructed below. The protocol first evaluates \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k \leftarrow g_n(x^n)$$\end{document}k←gn(xn), then samples an extractor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_s: \mathbb {A}^n \rightarrow \{0,1\}^k$$\end{document}Es:An→{0,1}k from a 2-universal family with seed s, and finally applies the extractor to the raw key register \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A^n$$\end{document}An and writes the k-bit result in the classical register \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$K_{x^n}$$\end{document}Kxn holding bitstrings of length \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g_n(x^n)$$\end{document}gn(xn). We write the map performing this as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {R}^{g_n}: {{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {D}}\,}}({{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {H}}\,}}_{X^nA^n}) \rightarrow {{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {D}}\,}}(\oplus _{x^n} {{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {H}}\,}}_{K_{x^n}} \otimes {{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {H}}\,}}_{S} )$$\end{document}Rgn:D(HXnAn)→D(⊕xnHKxn⊗HS) for this map.

Note that the length of the key is itself a random variable, whose distribution depends on the input state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho _{EQ^n}$$\end{document}ρEQn. Moreover, values of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x^n\in \mathbb {X}^n$$\end{document}xn∈Xn for which \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g_n(x^n)=0$$\end{document}gn(xn)=0, correspond to the cases where the protocol aborts.

Composable security For a given protocol \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {M}^n$$\end{document}Mn, post-processing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g_n$$\end{document}gn and input state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho _{Q^nE}$$\end{document}ρQnE satisfying (15), let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\oplus _{x^n \in \mathbb {X}^n} \rho _{K_{x^n}SE}^{x^n} = (\mathcal {R}^{g_n}\circ {{\,\mathrm{\mathcal {M}}\,}}^n \otimes {{\,\textrm{id}\,}}_E)(\rho _{Q^nE})$$\end{document}⊕xn∈XnρKxnSEn=(Rgn∘Mn⊗idE)(ρQnE) be the state obtained after applying the protocol and the post-processing. It is shown in [22] that the composable security level is given by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \epsilon (\mathcal {M}^n,g_n,\rho ) := \frac{1}{2} \sum _{x^n} \left\| \rho ^{x^n}_{K_{x^n}SE} - \tfrac{{{\,\textrm{id}\,}}_{K_{x^n}}}{|K_{x^n}|}\otimes \rho _{SE}^x \right\| _1\,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ϵ(Mn,gn,ρ):=12∑xnρKxnSEn-idKxn|Kxn|⊗ρSEx1.The full protocol is said to be \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\epsilon $$\end{document}ϵ-secure if for any input state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho _{Q^nE}$$\end{document}ρQnE satisfying (15), the final state satisfies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\epsilon (\mathcal {P},g_n,\rho )\le \epsilon $$\end{document}ϵ(P,gn,ρ)≤ϵ.

Asymptotic rate A protocol not only needs to be secure, it must also be efficient. Contray to the security condition, which must hold for any input state, the efficiency of the protocol is evaluated with respect to some “honest" input state, which is known in advance. Assume we are given honest input states \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho _{Q_t}^{\textrm{hon}}$$\end{document}ρQthon for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}t∈N, we consider the corresponding distributions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q^{\textrm{hon}}_{X_t} = \hbox {tr}_{A} \circ \mathcal {M}_t(\rho _{Q_t}^{\textrm{hon}})$$\end{document}qthon=trA∘Mt(ρQthon) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q^{\textrm{hon}}_{X^n} = \otimes _t q^{\textrm{hon}}_{X_t}$$\end{document}qnhon=⊗tqthon. Then the average key rate is given by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \textrm{rate}(\mathcal {M}^n, g_n, q^{\textrm{hon}}_{X^n}) = \frac{1}{n} \mathbb {E}_{x^n \sim q^{\textrm{hon}}_{X^n}}[g_n(x^n)]\,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}rate(Mn,gn,qnhon)=1nExn∼qnhon[gn(xn)].In particular we will be interested in the asymptotic limit \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n\rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}n→∞.

Time adaptive asymptotic key rates

For every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n\in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}n∈N, we want to build a post-processing function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g_n:\mathbb {X}^n \rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}gn:Xn→R, so that (a) the protocol is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\epsilon $$\end{document}ϵ-secure for any input state and (b) it achieves the largest possible key rate on average when applied to the state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\otimes _{t=1}^n \rho _{Q_t}^{\textrm{hon}}$$\end{document}⊗t=1nρQthon. How large can the average key rate be in the asymptotic limit \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n\rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}n→∞?

Time-invariant protocols This question was considered by Renner in [23], who considered the case where protocols do not vary in time, i.e. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {M}_t = \mathcal {M}$$\end{document}Mt=M, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {N}_t=\mathcal {N}$$\end{document}Nt=N, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau _i = \tau $$\end{document}τi=τ, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q^{\textrm{hon}}_{X_t} = q^{\textrm{hon}}_{X} $$\end{document}qthon=qXhon.
